## Continuation update, Round 425, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 425 audits the coefficient bookkeeping of the bridge-record cycle.  If
\(K_m,D_m,A_m,B_m\) are the source scale, prefix-record coefficient,
long-run coefficient, and bounded-successor budget at depth \(m\), then the
cycle gives
\[
  K_{m+1}=8K_mB_m,\qquad
  D_{m+1}=D_mB_m,\qquad
  A_{m+1}=A_m+D_{m+1}.
\]
Consequently
\[
  D_{m+1}/K_{m+1}=D_m/(8K_m),
\]
so each completed bridge-record cycle gains a factor \(1/8\) in the
coefficient-to-scale ratio.

This is a useful descent invariant, but it is not yet a proof of the target
theorem.  The next mathematical obligation is a scale-decay lemma for the
retained bridge-return, adjacent return-spread, and long-run profiles strong
enough to exploit this \(D_m/K_m\) gain after all finite \(j\)- and
\(h\)-summations.

## Continuation update, Round 426, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 426 corrects the Round 425 scale-decay target.  The spread-return event
\[
  \mathcal X^{\rm spr}_{j,K,p}
  =
  \left\{\#\{u\in A_{j,p}: \mathcal X_{u,0}\}
  >
  \theta L_H/(4K)\right\}
\]
is monotone increasing in \(K\), because larger \(K\) lowers the required
density of return levels.  Therefore a naive \(O(K^{-1})\) decay estimate for
\(\mathfrak X^{\rm rad,ret,spr}_{j,K}\) is not a valid target.

The corrected target is a fixed-density exponent estimate, phrased in terms
of
\[
  \delta=\theta/(4K),
\]
with explicit control of how the exponent degenerates as
\(\delta\downarrow0\).  The bridge-record coefficient identity remains useful
bookkeeping, but it cannot be promoted to an infinite-depth proof until such
density-rate estimates are available for spread-return and long-run profiles.

## Continuation update, Round 427, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 427 identifies a further quantitative obstruction to the corrected
fixed-density spread-return target.  Since
\[
  W_{\beta,p}=\lfloor\beta L_H\rfloor+O(1),
\]
a spread-return event with density
\[
  \delta=\theta/(4K)
\]
requires choosing \(\delta L_H\) return levels from a slab of length
\(\beta L_H+O(1)\).  A naive level-selection proof pays entropy
\[
  \beta H(\delta/\beta)L_H+o(L_H),
\]
which is order \(L_H\) and therefore visible in the logarithmic exponent.

The next target is now sharper: a spread-return exponent lemma must either
beat this entropy term or exploit nesting/dependence between exterior returns
so that the raw binomial cost is not paid.  This is necessary before the
bridge-record coefficient bookkeeping can become a proof mechanism.

## Continuation update, Round 428, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 428 records a deterministic laminarity lemma for exterior-return
intervals.  Two exterior-return intervals cannot cross in time: if one starts
inside another, it must also end inside it.  Moreover, nesting is radially
oriented: higher-level returns may lie inside lower-level returns, but not
conversely.

This supplies a possible way around the Round 427 binomial entropy barrier.
The next target is to turn laminarity into a quantitative return-level cost,
for example by proving that many return levels force either a long nested
chain or many disjoint excursions that can be charged separately.

## Continuation update, Round 429, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 429 converts exterior-return laminarity into a chain-antichain
dichotomy.  If a spread-return witness has \(N\) return levels, then for any
\(a\ge2\) it either has a nested chain of \(a\) exterior returns or has at
least \(N/(a-1)\) pairwise time-disjoint exterior-return intervals.

This decomposes the corrected spread-return target into a nested-return-chain
profile and a disjoint-return-excursion profile.  The next mathematical work
is to estimate these two profiles separately and see whether their combined
density-rate beats the entropy barrier identified in Round 427.

## Continuation update, Round 430, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 430 packages the Round 429 chain-antichain dichotomy as a profile
inequality:
\[
  \mathfrak X^{\rm rad,ret,spr}_{j,K}
  \le
  \mathfrak X^{\rm chain}_{j,K,a}
  +
  \mathfrak X^{\rm disj}_{j,K,a}.
\]
The chain profile asks for \(a\) nested exterior returns, while the disjoint
profile asks for at least
\[
  \left\lceil \theta L_H/(4K(a-1))\right\rceil
\]
pairwise time-disjoint exterior-return intervals.

## Continuation update, Round 431, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 431 audits the direct defect gain from spread returns.  If a path has
exterior returns through \(N\) distinct levels, then it has at least \(N\)
inward steps, so \(2I+H\ge2N\).  For spread returns this gives defect density
\(\theta/(2K)\), but the profile is already restricted to \(d>\lambda L_H\),
and \(\theta=\lambda/J_{\sigma,\beta}\).  Hence this direct defect count is
weaker than the existing far-tail threshold for \(K\ge1\).

The chain-disjoint reduction must therefore be exploited through structure
and entropy/factorization, not through a simple additional \(Q^d\) weight.

## Continuation update, Round 432, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 432 audits the quantitative limitation of the chain-antichain split. If
there are \(N\asymp L_H\) return levels, the dichotomy with parameter \(a\)
gives either a chain of length \(a\) or \(N/(a-1)\) disjoint returns.  This
does not by itself preserve a linear-size obstruction in both branches.

The next deterministic target is therefore a stronger, density-preserving
split, likely involving the radial span of nested chains: linear nested span
versus linearly many disjoint returns, with the small-span laminar residual
as the remaining combinatorial obstacle.

## Continuation update, Round 433, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 433 refines the span idea.  For any \(\eta>0\), a spread-return
laminar forest either has a nested pair of returns whose levels differ by at
least \(\eta L_H\), or it has at least
\[
  N/\lceil\eta L_H\rceil
\]
pairwise disjoint root intervals.

For fixed \(\eta\), this root count is only \(O(1)\), so the span split alone
still does not give a linear-density exponent.  The next target is a
tree-cost lemma for a laminar return tree with many selected levels inside a
short radial band.

## Continuation update, Round 434, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 434 stress-tests the tree-cost idea with a two-rail annular corridor.
A single self-avoiding path can go outward along one rail, cross once at the
top, and return along the other rail, producing a nested exterior return at
every intermediate level.  The radial cost is only the direct inward-step
cost plus one horizontal connector.

This shows that laminarity alone cannot provide the needed tree-cost lemma.
The estimate must use graph/percolation structure controlling annular
corridors.

## Continuation update, Round 435, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 435 reformulates the next target using annular exterior-return kernels.
For \(a,b\in S_{u-1}(x)\), let \(R_{p,u}^x(a,b)\) be the probability of an
open connection from \(a\) to \(b\) through the exterior of \(B_{u-1}(x)\)
with first and last edges entering \(S_u(x)\).  Time-disjoint return
intervals should be amenable to BK/Reimer factorization into products of
these annular kernels.

The next concrete target is an averaged annular-return contraction or
density-rate bound strong enough to beat the entropy cost from Round 427.
This reconnects the spread-return obstruction to finite-set escape and
boundary susceptibility estimates.

## Continuation update, Round 436, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 436 connects annular-return kernels to killed exterior boundary
energies.  For the exterior \(X_u=V\setminus B_{u-1}(x)\),
\[
  R_{p,u}^x(a,b)
  \le
  p^2\sum_{a'}\sum_{b'}\tau_p^{X_u}(a',b'),
\]
where \(a'\) and \(b'\) range over outward neighbours of \(a,b\) in
\(S_u(x)\).  Summing over \(a,b\in S_{u-1}(x)\) gives
\[
  \sum_{a,b}R_{p,u}^x(a,b)
  \le
  p^2\langle \nu_u,T_p^{X_u}\nu_u\rangle.
\]

This identifies the annular-return target as a ball-boundary version of the
finite-set boundary-energy obstruction.  The next analytic target is a
uniform contraction estimate for this killed exterior boundary energy.

## Continuation update, Round 437, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 437 extracts a sufficient annular contraction criterion.  Since
\[
  \|\nu_u\|_2^2\le \Delta^2|S_{u-1}(x)|,
\]
a uniform killed exterior bound
\[
  \|T_p^{X_u}\|_{2\to2}\le C_{\rm kill}
\]
would imply averaged annular contraction whenever
\[
  p^2\Delta^2 C_{\rm kill}<1.
\]

This is only a calibration: it is crude and potentially circular.  The next
target is a direct estimate of the boundary-vector energy
\(\langle\nu_u,T_p^{X_u}\nu_u\rangle\) for ball exteriors.

## Continuation update, Round 438, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 438 corrects the annular-kernel target for the radial-profile route.
The profiles are deterministic \(Q\)-weighted SAW counts, not probabilities of
annular connections.  For an exterior-return interval \(\eta\), the endpoints
have the same radius, so outward and inward counts agree and
\[
  2I(\eta)+H(\eta)=|\eta|.
\]
Thus the profile-compatible annular kernel is
\[
  A_{Q,u}^x(a,b)=\sum_{\eta:a\to b}Q^{|\eta|},
\]
summed over exterior-return SAWs, preferably truncated by the available defect
budget.  The percolation existence kernel from Rounds 435--437 is only an
analogy unless a separate multiplicity bound is supplied.

## Continuation update, Round 439, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 439 records that scalar annular averages do not control the marked
return contribution.  Deleting a return interval produces endpoint weights
\(\alpha,\beta\), and reinserting it costs
\[
  \langle\alpha,A_{Q,u}^x\beta\rangle.
\]
A bound on \(|S_{u-1}|^{-1}\sum_{a,b}A_{Q,u}^x(a,b)\) only controls diffuse
endpoint weights.  The next target must therefore be a Schur/operator
transfer estimate or an endpoint-spread dichotomy that sends concentrated
weights to a separate congestion/support profile.

## Continuation update, Round 440, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 440 defines the corrected deterministic boundary energy.  For
\[
  C_{Q,\le L}^{X_u}(y,z)
  =
  \sum_{\eta:y\to z,\ \eta\subset X_u,\ |\eta|\le L}Q^{|\eta|},
\]
the truncated annular return mass satisfies
\[
  \sum_{a,b} A_{Q,u,\le L}^x(a,b)
  \le
  Q^2\langle\nu_u,C_{Q,\le L-2}^{X_u}\nu_u\rangle.
\]
The weighted version pushes endpoint weights \(f,g\) from \(S_{u-1}\) to
\(\widehat f,\widehat g\) on \(S_u\) and bounds
\[
  \langle f,A_{Q,u,\le L}^xg\rangle
  \le
  Q^2\langle\widehat f,C_{Q,\le L-2}^{X_u}\widehat g\rangle.
\]
This is the current analytic target for the spread-return branch.

## Continuation update, Round 441, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 441 reduces weighted endpoint insertions to annular cut profiles.  For
the truncated transfer matrix \(A=A_{Q,u,\le L}^x\),
\[
  \langle f,Ag\rangle
\]
is controlled by dyadic endpoint level sets \(U_i,V_j\) and cut masses
\[
  A(U_i,V_j)=\sum_{a\in U_i,b\in V_j}A(a,b).
\]
Thus a large marked-return contribution means either endpoint congestion in
the skeleton weights or a large unweighted annular cut between boundary
subsets.

## Continuation update, Round 442, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 442 rewrites annular cuts as subset boundary energies.  For
\(U,V\subseteq S_{u-1}(x)\),
\[
  A_{Q,u,\le L}^x(U,V)
  \le
  Q^2\langle\nu_U,C_{Q,\le L-2}^{X_u}\nu_V\rangle.
\]
The current target is now a restricted subset-energy estimate for boundary
level sets, weaker than a global \(L^2\) bound but stronger than whole-sphere
averaging.

## Continuation update, Round 443, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 443 calibrates the corrected annular transfer against the trivial
bounded-degree Schur estimate:
\[
  \sum_b A_{Q,u,\le L}^x(a,b)
  \le
  C_\Delta\sum_{\ell=2}^L (Q(\Delta-1))^\ell .
\]
Since the subdivision criterion works with \(Q>\pc(G)\), and
\(\pc(G)\ge(\Delta-1)^{-1}\), this row-sum bound may grow exponentially in
\(L\).  The annular-transfer route must therefore use exact-sphere averaging,
subset cuts, laminar nesting, or graph-specific operator geometry.

## Continuation update, Round 444, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 444 observes that a root exterior-return interval covers a contiguous
block of return levels.  If \(I\) is a return through level \(u\) and reaches
maximum radius \(M(I)\), then it generates returns through every
\(w\in\{u,\ldots,M(I)\}\).  Thus spread-return levels are covered by the
coverage blocks of the root intervals in the laminar forest.

This changes the entropy count: few root intervals give interval-cover
entropy, not arbitrary subset entropy; many root intervals give many
time-disjoint annular returns.

## Continuation update, Round 445, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 445 derives the coverage-entropy split.  If \(N=\delta L_H\) return
levels in a slab of length \(\beta L_H\) are covered by fewer than
\(\kappa L_H\) root coverage intervals, the number of coverage patterns has
exponent
\[
  \beta H(2\kappa/\beta)L_H+o(L_H),
\]
while root-span defect contributes \(Q^{2N}\).  The few-root branch is
plausibly controlled when
\[
  \beta H(2\kappa/\beta)<2\delta|\log Q|.
\]
The complementary branch has linearly many time-disjoint root returns and is
the right place for the annular subset-energy estimates.

## Continuation update, Round 446, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 446 packages the coverage split as a profile inequality.  For
\(\kappa>0\),
\[
  \mathcal X^{\rm spr}_{j,K,p}
  \subseteq
  \mathcal X^{\rm rootdisj}_{j,\kappa,p}
  \cup
  \mathcal X^{\rm cov}_{j,K,\kappa,p},
\]
where the first branch has at least \(\kappa L_H\) pairwise time-disjoint root
returns, and the second branch covers \(>\delta L_H\) slab levels with fewer
than \(\kappa L_H\) root coverage intervals.  This gives the corresponding
coefficient-free radial profile bound.

## Continuation update, Round 447, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 447 corrects the few-root bookkeeping.  Deleting a root return interval
leaves a same-sphere jump, which should be charged as one horizontal defect
unit.  If \(m\) root intervals with coverage lengths \(h_r\) are deleted,
\[
  d(\gamma)-d(\gamma^\circ)
  \ge
  2\sum_r h_r-m.
\]
Thus in the few-root branch the net gain over the jump skeleton is
\((2\delta-\kappa)L_H+O(1)\), and the honest entropy condition is
\[
  \beta H(2\kappa/\beta)<(2\delta-\kappa)|\log Q|.
\]
The missing lemma is now explicit: control same-sphere jump-skeleton profiles
at logarithmic scale.

## Continuation update, Round 448, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 448 identifies a caveat in the jump-skeleton approach.  Same-sphere
jumps cannot be counted as free clique edges on spheres: at radius
\(u\asymp L_H\), each jump may have \(O(L_H)\) endpoint entropy, and
\(\kappa L_H\) jumps would create an \(O(\kappa L_H^2)\) loss.  Endpoint
choices must stay coupled to annular transfer/cut estimates.

## Continuation update, Round 449, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 449 summarizes the current obligations.  The many-root branch needs a
product estimate for linearly many corrected annular transfers.  The few-root
branch has low radial coverage entropy and net defect gain, but still needs
endpoint control.  The unified target is an annular cut-energy estimate whose
failure extracts boundary subsets with large truncated exterior
\(Q\)-weighted SAW energy.

## Continuation update, Round 450, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 450 connects large annular cut energy to restricted exterior SAW
operator mass.  If
\[
  E=Q^2\langle\nu_U,C_{Q,\le L}^{X_u}\nu_V\rangle
\]
is large, then for some \(\ell\le L\),
\[
  \|C_\ell^{X_u}\|_{2\to2}
  \gtrsim
  \frac{E}{(L+1)Q^{\ell+2}\Delta^2\sqrt{|U||V|}}.
\]
So failure of cut control gives a coherent killed-SAW operator obstruction.

## Continuation update, Round 451, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 451 records a sufficient condition: a uniform killed exterior SAW
operator bound
\[
  \|C_\ell^{X_u}\|_{2\to2}\le e^{o(\ell)}a^\ell,\qquad a<1/Q,
\]
would imply an annular cut estimate
\[
  A_{Q,u,\le L}^x(U,V)\le C_Q\sqrt{|U||V|}.
\]
This calibrates the current target as a localized killed-SAW \(L^2\) problem,
close to the earlier global SAW operator-gap route.

## Continuation update, Round 452, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 452 names the annular boundary SAW exponent
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(G)
  =
  \limsup_\ell\sup_{x,u,U,V}
  \left(
    \frac{\langle\nu_U,C_\ell^{X_u}\nu_V\rangle}{\sqrt{|U||V|}}
  \right)^{1/\ell}.
\]
If \(Q\Gamma_{\rm ann}(G)<1\), then the annular cut estimate
\[
  A_{Q,u,\le L}^x(U,V)\le C_Q\sqrt{|U||V|}
\]
holds uniformly.  This is weaker and more targeted than the global SAW
operator-gap condition.

## Continuation update, Round 453, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 453 shows that annular cut control bounds weighted insertions by
endpoint \(L^2\) norms:
\[
  \langle f,A_{Q,u,\le L}^xg\rangle
  \le
  e^{o(L_H)}C\|f\|_2\|g\|_2.
\]
The subexponential factor comes from dyadic endpoint levels for skeleton
weights of length \(O(L_H)\).

## Continuation update, Round 454, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 454 identifies endpoint \(L^2\) as a two-replica boundary congestion
profile: \(\sum_a f(a)^2\) counts ordered pairs of skeleton prefixes ending at
the same boundary vertex.  The spread-return branch is now split into an
annular boundary SAW gap problem and an endpoint two-replica congestion
problem.

## Continuation update, Round 455, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 455 compares endpoint congestion with the earlier finite-set
two-replica profile.  The old object uses near-critical percolation weights
\(\tau_p\), while the new endpoint weights are deterministic \(Q\)-weighted
skeleton counts with \(Q>\pc(G)\).  A counting-to-percolation bridge or a new
deterministic endpoint profile is still needed.

## Continuation update, Round 456, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 456 extracts a support-congestion set from endpoint \(L^2\) failure. If
\(f\) is a skeleton endpoint weight, some dyadic boundary subset \(U\) has
\[
  \frac{(\sum_{a\in U}f(a))^2}{|U|}
  \ge
  \frac{\|f\|_2^2}{4N_f}.
\]
For skeletons of length \(O(L_H)\), \(N_f=O(L_H)\), so the loss is
subexponential.

## Continuation update, Round 457, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 457 summarizes the reduced obstruction map: the spread-return branch now
requires annular boundary SAW transfer control, deterministic endpoint
support-congestion control, and possibly a bridge from deterministic support
profiles to percolative finite-set/two-replica machinery.

## Continuation update, Round 458, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 458 calibrates the \(Q\)-window.  Since in the subdivision route
\(Q=q^2\) and \(q\downarrow\pc(\operatorname{Sub}(G))\), the annular boundary
condition should be stated as
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(G)<1/\pc(G).
\]
This is the annular-return analogue of the global SAW operator-gap condition,
but restricted to killed exterior boundary-to-boundary returns.

## Continuation update, Round 459, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 459 packages the current conditional endpoint.  The spread-return
branch would be controlled by: an annular boundary gap
\(\Gamma_{\rm ann}<1/Q\), endpoint \(L^2\) insertion control, and a
subcritical deterministic endpoint support-congestion estimate.  The latter
is the remaining missing input in this branch.

## Continuation update, Round 460, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 460 stress-tests the annular exponent on \(T_d\square H\).  A staircase
return alternates geodesic motion in \(H\) with inward tree steps, staying
outside the ball until it returns to the boundary.  This gives
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square H)\ge \sqrt{g_{\rm geo}(H)}.
\]

## Continuation update, Round 461, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 461 compares the product stress tests.  The first annular staircase
lower bound lacks the tree-branching term present in the global SAW operator
lower bound \(\sqrt{d-1}+\mu(H)\).  Round 462 corrects this with a stronger
out-and-back construction, but the annular target remains weaker than the
global product SAW route.

## Continuation update, Round 462, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 462 adds a product out-and-back lower bound.  If \(H\) has an edge, then
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square H)\ge \sqrt{d-1}.
\]
Together with the factor-geodesic staircase,
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square H)
  \ge
  \max\{\sqrt{d-1},\sqrt{g_{\rm geo}(H)}\}.
\]

## Continuation update, Round 463, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 463 notes that the corrected product lower bound still does not refute
the desired annular gap, since \(1/\pc(T_d\square H)\ge d-1>\sqrt{d-1}\).
The next product calibration is an upper bound for \(\Gamma_{\rm ann}\) on
\(T_d\square\mathbb Z^r\).

## Continuation update, Round 464, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 464 identifies \(T_d\square K_2\) as the first exact annular
upper-bound test.  The current lower bound is
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square K_2)\ge\sqrt{d-1},
\]
and even proving \(\Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square K_2)<d-1\) would support the
annular gap calibration.

## Continuation update, Round 465, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 465 checks the crude radial-excess transfer for \(T_d\square K_2\).  It
overcounts badly, with excursion spectral radius of order \(2\sqrt{2d}\),
because it forgets layer-wise self-avoidance.  A useful upper bound must
remember stack/spine information in the tree projection.

## Continuation update, Round 466, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 466 isolates the principal branch-switch recursion in
\(T_d\square K_2\):
\[
  S(z)=z+(d-1)z^2S(z).
\]
This gives exponent \(\sqrt{d-1}\), matching the out-and-back lower bound.
It is not yet a full upper bound for \(\Gamma_{\rm ann}\), but it identifies
the main recursive return type.

## Continuation update, Round 467, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 467 identifies the exterior components of \(T_d\square K_2\).  Outside
\(B_{u-1}((o,0))\), components are two-port descendant cones indexed by tree
vertices \(t\) at depth \(u-1\), with boundary ports
\[
  A_t=(t,0),\qquad B_t=(\operatorname{par}(t),1).
\]
Every exterior return through level \(u\) stays in one such component.

## Continuation update, Round 468, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 468 computes the exact two-port transfer.  With \(q=d-1\),
\[
  F(z)=z^2+qz^3\frac{z}{1-qz^2}
      =z^2+\frac{qz^4}{1-qz^2},
\]
so transfer coefficients grow like \(q^{n/2}\).

## Continuation update, Round 469, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 469 concludes the first exact non-tree annular calibration:
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square K_2)=\sqrt{d-1}.
\]
Since \(T_d\square K_2\) contains \(T_d\), this gives
\(\Gamma_{\rm ann}<1/\pc\) for all \(d\ge3\).

## Continuation update, Round 470, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 470 generalizes the product calibration target.  For finite \(F\), the
exterior of \(T_d\square F\) decomposes into bounded-width decorated tree
cones with only \(O_{F,d}(1)\) boundary ports, including both descendant-side
and parent-side ports.  This reduces \(\Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square F)\) to a
finite-state boundary-to-boundary SAW transfer target.  The finite-fiber
goal is to compare its exponent \(\rho_F(d-1)\) with
\(1/\pc(T_d\square F)\).

## Continuation update, Round 471, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 471 gives an explicit finite-state transfer for \(T_d\square F\).
States record the used subset of a finite bag, local degrees, connectivity
pairings of open path ends, and boundary terminals.  The resulting vector
generating function satisfies a finite recursive system
\[
  \mathbf G(z)=\Phi_F(z,\mathbf G(z);d-1).
\]
The finite-fiber task is now to prove the selected singularity exponent
\(\rho_F(d-1)\) is strictly below \(1/\pc(T_d\square F)\).  The stronger
bound \(<d-1\) remains a useful low-width sufficient condition but is not the
natural target for arbitrary finite fibers.

## Continuation update, Round 472, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 472 records two corrections to the finite-fiber roadmap: component
ports include both descendant-side ports and parent-side ports, and the
finite-fiber transfer exponent should be compared with
\(1/\pc(T_d\square F)\), not uniformly with \(d-1\).  The \(K_2\) calculation
remains a low-width case where the stronger \(<d-1\) bound holds.

## Continuation update, Round 473, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 473 formulates \(\pc(T_d\square F)\) by a finite multitype branching
matrix.  Types are nonempty fiber subsets \(A\subseteq V(F)\); a child type
is obtained by open tree entries from \(A\) followed by closure under open
edges in \(F\).  With mean matrix \(M_p(A,B)=(d-1)K_p(A,B)\), the threshold
is \(\pc=\inf\{p:r(M_p)>1\}\).  The finite-fiber annular target is now the
finite comparison \(\rho_F(d-1)<1/p_*\), where \(r(M_{p_*})=1\).

## Continuation update, Round 474, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 474 stress-tests the strong finite-fiber target on
\(T_d\square K_m\).  Using disjoint outbound and inbound fiber sets of sizes
\(\alpha,\beta\), annular returns give the lower bound
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square K_m)
  \ge
  \max_{\alpha+\beta\le m-1}
  \bigl((d-1)(\alpha-2)!(\beta-2)!\bigr)^{1/(\alpha+\beta)}.
\]
For \(\alpha,\beta\asymp m/2\), this is order \(m\), so the uniform bound
\(<d-1\) is not the right finite-fiber target.  The comparison with
\(1/\pc(T_d\square K_m)\) remains the relevant one.

## Continuation update, Round 475, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 475 records the matching percolation scale for clique fibers.  For
\(p=C/m\), each clique bag has the \(G(m,C/m)\) giant when \(C>1\).  A
giant parent type hits a child giant through matching fiber tree edges with
probability about \(1-\exp(-C\theta(C)^2)\), so for \(C=C(d)\) large enough
the coarse giant-type branching process is supercritical.  Hence
\[
  \pc(T_d\square K_m)\le (C(d)+o(1))/m,
\]
and \(1/\pc\) is also linear in \(m\).  This supports the corrected
finite-fiber comparison with \(1/\pc\), while not proving the annular gap.

## Continuation update, Round 476, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 476 identifies \(T_d\square\mathbb Z\) as the next product calibration.
Finite-fiber products have bounded-port exterior cones, but outside a ball in
\(T_d\square\mathbb Z\), all tree branches communicate through high
\(\mathbb Z\)-levels.  A self-avoiding annular path can switch branches at
many distinct heights, so the finite-fiber recursion does not apply.  The
next target is a half-plane transfer in \((h,k)=(d_T(o,v),|z|)\), with
self-avoidance memory for tree vertices at each height.

## Continuation update, Round 477, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 477 isolates a product-shuffle fork for \(T_d\square\mathbb Z\).  The
global SAW operator has lower bound \(\sqrt{d-1}+1\), but finite-fiber
annular transfers do not realize additive shuffle entropy.  For the one-ended
product, the open question is whether unbounded portal heights allow an
annular product-shuffle family with
\(\Gamma_{\rm ann}\ge\sqrt{d-1}+1\), or whether the exterior/sphere
constraint forces a weaker return-coupled exponent.

## Continuation update, Round 478, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 478 constructs a portal-staircase lower bound in
\(T_d\square\mathbb Z\).  A path goes from depth \(u\) to \((o,u)\) by
Catalan interleavings of inward tree and upward line steps, then descends a
different branch by Catalan interleavings of outward tree and downward line
steps.  With \(\asymp(d-1)^u\) terminal branches and length \(4u\), this gives
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)\ge 2(d-1)^{1/8}.
\]
Together with the tree out-and-back family, the current lower calibration is
\(\max\{\sqrt{d-1},2(d-1)^{1/8}\}\).  This proves portal heights add annular
entropy, though it does not settle the possible \(\sqrt{d-1}+1\) shuffle
lower bound.  Round 479 improves this lower calibration using large endpoint
sets on both sides of the annular cut norm.

## Continuation update, Round 479, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 479 strengthens the portal-staircase lower bound.  Taking \(U\) and
\(V\) to be large boundary sets in two disjoint principal branches gives
\(|U|\asymp |V|\asymp(d-1)^u\); the Catalan path through \((o,u)\) contributes
\(C_u^2\) for each endpoint pair.  After \(\sqrt{|U||V|}\) normalization and
length \(4u\), this yields
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)\ge 2(d-1)^{1/4}.
\]
The current lower calibration is therefore
\[
  \max\{\sqrt{d-1},2(d-1)^{1/4}\}.
\]

## Continuation update, Round 480, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 480 optimizes the one-portal staircase family.  With endpoint tree
depth \(r=\lambda u\) and portal height \(M=\mu u\), the one-leg binomial
exponent gives rate
\[
  \exp\{H(t)+(t/2)\log(d-1)\},\qquad t\le1/2.
\]
This is maximized at \(t=1/2\), so the best one-portal exponent is
\[
  2(d-1)^{1/4}.
\]
Therefore a full \(\sqrt{d-1}+1\) product-shuffle lower bound, if true, must
come from repeated or distributed portals rather than a single high portal.

## Continuation update, Round 481, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 481 isolates a repeated root-portal bubble transfer in
\(T_d\square\mathbb Z\).  A bubble moves out to tree depth \(r\) at high line
height, takes one upward step, and returns inward while interleaving the
remaining \(s-1\) upward steps.  Optimizing the rate of
\[
  q^r\binom{r+s-1}{r}
\]
over length \(2r+s\) gives
\[
  \lambda_q=\frac{1+\sqrt{1+4q}}{2},\qquad q=d-1.
\]
This is only a high-root transfer rate, not yet an annular lower bound:
after many bubbles, the path must still descend back to the fixed annular
boundary at comparable cost.

## Continuation update, Round 482, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 482 corrects the repeated-bubble construction by adding the final
descent to the boundary.  With bubble totals \(R,S\) and terminal tree depth
\(T\), the corrected variational lower-bound expression is
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)
  \ge
  \exp\!\left[
    \sup
    \frac{
      R\log q
      +(R+S)H(R/(R+S))
      +(T/2)\log q
      +(S+2T)H(T/(S+2T))
    }{2(R+S+T)}
  \right].
\]
The previous \(\lambda_q\) term cannot be used without this exit cost.  The
proven lower calibration remains at least
\(\max\{\sqrt{d-1},2(d-1)^{1/4}\}\) pending optimization of the corrected
expression.

## Continuation update, Round 483, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 483 extracts a clean paid-exit subfamily from the corrected variational
target.  A bubble of depth \(R\) and height gain \(S=aR\), followed by a
sublinear fresh-column descent, has rate
\[
  \exp\left\{
    \frac{\log q+(1+a)H(1/(1+a))}{2(1+a)}
  \right\}.
\]
Optimizing gives \(a=1/q\) and rate \(\sqrt{q+1}=\sqrt d\).  The current
explicit lower calibration for \(T_d\square\mathbb Z\) is therefore
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)
  \ge
  \max\{2(d-1)^{1/4},\sqrt d\}.
\]

## Continuation update, Round 484, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 484 optimizes the corrected repeated-bubble family by summing its
generating function.  With \(q=d-1\) and \(t\) marking half-length,
\[
  F_q(t)=
  \frac{1}{(1-qt)\sqrt{1-4\sqrt q\,t}}
  \frac{1}{1-tA(\sqrt q\,t)/(1-qt)},
  \qquad
  A(x)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4x}}{2x}.
\]
The optimized rate for this family is \(\Lambda_q=t_q^{-1/2}\), where
\(t_q\) is the first positive singularity, including the possible interior
solution \(tA(\sqrt q\,t)/(1-qt)=1\).  This gives the refined lower bound
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)\ge \Lambda_{d-1}.
\]

## Continuation update, Round 485, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 485 gives the explicit phase formula for \(\Lambda_q\).  With
\(s=\sqrt q\), the interior singularity produces
\[
  y_-(s)=
  \frac{
    2s^3-s^2-1
    -
    \sqrt{(2s^3-s^2-1)^2-4(s-1)s^5}
  }{2(s-1)}.
\]
It beats the branch singularity exactly when \(s\ge2+\sqrt2\), i.e.
\(q\ge6+4\sqrt2\).  Therefore
\[
  \Lambda_q=
  \begin{cases}
    2q^{1/4}, & q<6+4\sqrt2,\\
    \sqrt{y_-(\sqrt q)}, & q\ge6+4\sqrt2.
  \end{cases}
\]
For integer \(q=d-1\), this transition begins at \(q=12\).

## Continuation update, Round 486, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 486 adds a column-cluster percolation comparison for
\(T_d\square\mathbb Z\).  Revealing the vertical cluster of size \(N\) in a
reached column gives forward Galton-Watson mean
\[
  m_q(p)=
  q\left(1-\frac{(1-p)^3}{(1-p+p^2)^2}\right).
\]
For \(q=2\), the solution of \(m_q(p)=1\) has inverse
\[
  1/p_{\rm col}=3.0523003446\ldots,
\]
which is above the current \(\Lambda_2=2\cdot2^{1/4}\).  For integer
\(q\ge3\), the embedded-tree bound \(1/\pc\ge q\) is already above
\(\Lambda_q\).  The current portal lower bounds therefore do not obstruct
the desired annular gap on \(T_d\square\mathbb Z\).

## Continuation update, Round 487, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 487 identifies the sharp upper target
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)\le\sqrt{d-1}+1.
\]
This would imply the desired annular gap for all \(d\ge3\): for \(d-1\ge3\)
it is below the embedded-tree inverse threshold, and for \(d-1=2\) it is
below the column-cluster inverse threshold from Round 486.  A sufficient
operator estimate is the tree-line split
\[
  \frac{\langle \nu_U,C_{n,a}^{X_u}\nu_V\rangle}{\sqrt{|U||V|}}
  \le
  e^{o(n)}\binom{n}{a}(d-1)^{a/2}.
\]
The remaining obstacle is proving this Haagerup-type square-root tree
branching while controlling tree backtracks at different line heights.

## Continuation update, Round 488, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 488 isolates the stack-charge lemma needed for the tree-line split.
For a projected tree walk with \(a\) tree steps and endpoint displacement
\(D\), raw branch choices cost \(q^{(a+D)/2}\), while endpoint \(L^2\)
averaging should supply \(q^{D/2}\), leaving the desired \(q^{a/2}\).  The
remaining Catalan push-pop entropy must be charged to line-height separation:
a pop over a tree edge at the same height would violate self-avoidance.  This
lemma would imply the Round 487 estimate and hence
\(\Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)\le\sqrt q+1\).

## Continuation update, Round 489, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 489 corrects the Round 488 stack-charge target.  The line-height trace
can itself have exponential entropy: with \(b\) line steps split into
\(r\le a+1\) monotone vertical runs, a crude count is
\[
  \sum_{r\le a+1}2^r\binom{b-1}{r-1}.
\]
Thus the missing upper lemma must jointly control tree stack schedules,
vertical run decomposition, and the annular excess excursion; the line trace
cannot be treated as subexponential by itself.

## Continuation update, Round 490, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 490 finds a low-branching obstruction to the \(\sqrt q+1\) upper
target.  Fresh-column paths that move only outward in the tree and take
monotone vertical runs have block inventory
\[
  B_q(z,w)=
  \sqrt q\,z\,w
  \left(1+\frac{zw}{1-zw}+\frac{z/w}{1-z/w}\right),
\]
whose excursion critical equations give lower rate
\[
  \Xi_q=\sqrt{1+4\sqrt q}.
\]
This exceeds \(\sqrt q+1\) for \(q=2,3\).  The corrected sufficient upper
target is therefore
\[
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)
  \le
  \max\{\sqrt q+1,\sqrt{1+4\sqrt q}\},
\]
which still lies below the available inverse-\(\pc\) bounds for every
integer \(q=d-1\ge2\).

## Continuation update, Round 491, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 491 sets the corrected master upper target
\[
  M_q=\max\{\sqrt q+1,\sqrt{1+4\sqrt q}\},
  \qquad
  \Gamma_{\rm ann}(T_d\square\mathbb Z)\le M_q.
\]
This target still implies the annular gap for every integer \(q=d-1\ge2\).
The missing proof must jointly control stack-return behavior and fresh-column
vertical-run excursions, and show that alternating between the two mechanisms
does not produce a larger exponential scale.

## Continuation update, Round 492, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 492 separates tree branching from height-order entropy.  For an
annular SAW, let \(S(\gamma)\) be the support tree of its tree projection and
let \(K=|E(S(\gamma))|\).  If \(a\) is the number of tree steps and \(D\) the
tree endpoint displacement, then \(K\le(a+D)/2\).  Branch choices cost
\(q^K\), while endpoint \(L^2\) averaging supplies \(q^{D/2}\), leaving
\(q^{a/2}\).  Thus the remaining upper problem is to count height-labeled
support-tree traversals under self-avoidance and the annular excess
constraint.

## Continuation update, Round 493, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 493 tests the naive fixed-height run decomposition.  Free alternation
of tree runs with inventory \(\sqrt q\,z/(1-\sqrt q\,z)\) and vertical runs
with inventory \(2z/(1-z)\) gives rate
\[
  R_{\rm run}(q)=
  \frac{\sqrt q+1+\sqrt{q+6\sqrt q+1}}{2}.
\]
This is too large, e.g. \(R_{\rm run}(3)=3.262\ldots>3\).  The failure shows
that an upper proof must track signed tree-depth changes and vertical-run
signs through the annular excess excursion, not merely alternate run lengths.

## Continuation update, Round 494, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 494 tests signed run tracking and finds it still badly overcounts.
The signed tree-run inventory
\[
  T_s(z,w)=\frac{1}{(1-szw)(1-sz/w)}-1,\qquad s=\sqrt q,
\]
combined freely with vertical runs gives rates \(6.4859\ldots\) for \(q=2\)
and \(7.4914\ldots\) for \(q=3\), far above the corrected target.  The
failure is support-memory loss: tree runs cannot be treated as fresh after
edges and columns have already been used at specific heights.

## Continuation update, Round 495, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 495 proposes a rooted cone height-kernel architecture.  For a parent
edge into a descendant cone, define \(E_z(h,h')\) as the generating function
of self-avoiding excursions that enter at height \(h\) and exit at height
\(h'\), with no intermediate use of the parent edge.  Repeated crossings of
the same tree edge are therefore tracked by distinct heights.  Inside the
cone root column, disjoint vertical intervals and child-cone excursions give
an interval-renewal transform \(E_z\preceq\mathcal F_q(z,E_z)\).  Proving this
transform is finite for \(z<M_q^{-1}\) is the next concrete upper-bound
route.

## Continuation update, Round 496, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 496 corrects the Round 495 cone-kernel architecture.  A self-avoiding
path can use the same child cone more than once at different heights, so a
two-point kernel \(E_z(h,h')\) is insufficient.  The recursive object must be
a multi-port cone kernel \(E_z(\mathcal P;A)\), where \(A\) is a finite
ordered set of boundary heights and \(\mathcal P\) records the connectivity
pattern inside the cone.  The next upper-bound object is therefore a polymer
norm over finite height sets with span/gap penalties, not just an
\(\ell^2(\mathbb Z)\) kernel norm.

## Continuation update, Round 497, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 497 refines the multi-port object into root-column trace words.  A cone
path determines distinct root-column heights
\(h_0,\ldots,h_m\), and each gap is labeled either by a vertical interval or
by a child-cone piece.  For each child label, the induced subword is passed
recursively to that child cone in global path order.  The concrete upper
object is now a trace-word partition function \(Z_z(\mathcal W)\), summed
over words satisfying distinct-height, disjoint-interval, recursive
feasibility, and annular-excess constraints.

## Continuation update, Round 498, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 498 corrects the trace-word object: the recorded heights are compressed
portal heights, while vertical labels are maximal root-column runs.  For a
fixed ordered portal set, root-column self-avoidance forces the vertical runs
to be a hard-core matching of adjacent height gaps.  This removes the free
signed vertical-run entropy that invalidated earlier upper attempts.  The
remaining trace-word problem is to control the time order of portals and the
shuffle of child-cone subwords under the annular exterior constraint.

## Continuation update, Round 499, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 499 refines the recursive child object.  A child label receives an
ordered packet of boundary-to-boundary pieces, not a single continuous trace.
The cone partition function should therefore be \(E_z(\Pi)\) for an ordered
multi-port packet \(\Pi=((a_1,b_1),\ldots,(a_k,b_k))\).  The root trace
transform shuffles the packets belonging to the \(q\) children together with
vertical runs; using an exponential-generating norm in the packet size
absorbs the resulting multinomial factors.  The remaining target is a
gap/excess weight \(\Phi\) that makes this packet transform close for
\(z<M_q^{-1}\).

## Continuation update, Round 500, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 500 calibrates the excess weight in the packet norm.  The weight must
recover the fresh-column block inequality
\[
  B_q(z,w)=
  \sqrt q\,zw
  \left(1+\frac{zw}{1-zw}+\frac{z/w}{1-z/w}\right)<1
\]
for every \(z<M_q^{-1}\), where
\[
  M_q=\max\{\sqrt q+1,\sqrt{1+4\sqrt q}\}.
\]
This fixes the role of the annular-excess multiplier \(w\).  The candidate
norm is now an exponential-generating packet norm weighted by the sum of
boundary-port excesses, leaving the ordered gap factor \(\Phi\) as the main
unsolved component.

## Continuation update, Round 501, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 501 proposes a concrete two-component gap factor for the packet norm.
The factor \(\alpha^{\sum |a_i-b_i|}\) controls summation over child
boundary-pair spans, while
\[
  G_\eta(A)=\prod_i(1+\eta^{x_{i+1}-x_i})
\]
dominates the hard-core matching partition of root-column vertical intervals
on the ordered portal set \(A=\{x_0<\cdots<x_m\}\).  The current technical
target is to close the root trace transform in the weighted norm with
parameters \(w,\xi,\alpha,\eta\) whenever \(z<M_q^{-1}\).

## Continuation update, Round 502, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 502 explicitly optimizes the fresh-column excess multiplier.  Writing
\(s=\sqrt q\), the minimizer of \(B_q(z,w)\) is
\[
  w_*(z)=\frac{2z}{1+z^2},
\]
and
\[
  \min_w B_q(z,w)=\frac{4s z^2}{1-z^2}.
\]
Thus \(B_q(z,w)<1\) is possible exactly for
\[
  z<1/\sqrt{1+4s}.
\]
This recovers the fresh-column threshold and supplies an explicit \(w\) for
the packet norm at every \(z<M_q^{-1}\).  The remaining obstruction is the
gap/multi-packet closure, not the excess multiplier.

## Continuation update, Round 503, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 503 states the recursion at packet level.  A packet
\(\Pi=((a_1,b_1),\ldots,(a_k,b_k))\) is realized by \(k\) mutually avoiding
cone pieces, so the root-column data is an ordered family of compressed trace
words, one for each boundary pair, sharing the same root column.  Validity of
portal heights and vertical intervals is global across the family, and child
packets are obtained by collecting child-labeled pairs from all pieces in
global order.  The closure problem is therefore a polymer estimate for trace
families, with the single trace word as the \(k=1\) case.

## Continuation update, Round 504, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 504 attempted to rule out a crude scalar row-sum closure.  The
diagnostic collapsed the pair-span kernel with the optimized excess
multiplier and obtained
\[
  C_{\rm row}(q,z)
  =
  q\left(\frac{2z}{1+z^2}\right)^2\frac{2z}{1-z}.
\]
Round 505 corrects this diagnostic: it omitted the two crossing-edge costs
\(z^2\) per child atom.  The Round 504 obstruction should therefore not be
used.

## Continuation update, Round 505, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 505 corrects the scalar child-atom inventory by restoring the two
parent-child crossing-edge costs.  The corrected inventory is
\[
  A_q(z)
  =
  qz^2\left(\frac{2z}{1+z^2}\right)^2\frac{2z}{1-z}
  =
  \frac{8qz^5}{(1-z)(1+z^2)^2}.
\]
At \(z=M_q^{-1}\), this is less than \(0.173\) for every integer \(q\ge2\).
Thus child-label proliferation and height-jump row sums are not the
obstruction once crossing edges are charged.  The next task is to use this
small atom inventory together with the hard-core adjacent-gap gas and packet
norm to build an actual closure estimate.

## Continuation update, Round 506, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 506 tests the corrected scalar one-trace skeleton.  Let \(A_q(z)\) be
the corrected child-atom inventory and \(U(z)=2z/(1-z)\) the crude vertical
run inventory.  Keeping only the rule that two maximal vertical runs cannot
be consecutive gives the transfer matrix
\[
  \begin{pmatrix}A_q(z)&U(z)\\ A_q(z)&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
At \(z=M_q^{-1}\), its spectral radius is uniformly less than one; the worst
integer case is \(q=2\), with radius \(0.561834\ldots\).  This suggests that
the corrected scalar trace skeleton is not an obstruction.  The remaining
challenge is to lift the estimate to the full packet-level root transform.

## Continuation update, Round 507, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 507 makes the scalar trace skeleton endpoint-span resolved.  Writing
\[
  c_q(z)=qz^2\left(\frac{2z}{1+z^2}\right)^2,
\]
monotone portal subdivisions have span growth
\[
  \lambda(c)=\frac{2+c+\sqrt{c^2+4c}}{2}.
\]
At \(z=M_q^{-1}\), the base \(z\lambda(c_q(z))\) is below one for all integer
\(q\ge2\), with worst tested value \(0.559622\ldots\) at \(q=2\).  The packet
span parameter should therefore be chosen with slack
\[
  z\lambda(c_q(z))<\alpha<1,
\]
which addresses the monotone-subdivision endpoint issue left by row-sum
estimates.

## Continuation update, Round 508, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 508 checks an explicit packet supersolution in the monotone trace
model.  If \(C\) is the per-packet constant and \(\alpha\) the span weight,
the normalized one-trace transform is bounded by
\[
  H_{q,z,\alpha}(C)
  =
  \frac{P(1+Cc_q+Cc_qP)}{1-Cc_qP(1+P)},
  \qquad P=\frac{z/\alpha}{1-z/\alpha}.
\]
At \(z=M_q^{-1}\), the uniform choice \(\alpha=0.9\), \(C=2\) satisfies
\(H_{q,z,0.9}(2)<2\) for every integer \(q\ge2\).  This closes the simplified
monotone packet model and leaves nonmonotone height traces plus the
adjacent-gap gas as the next issues.

## Continuation update, Round 509, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 509 tests a two-sided exponential tilt for nonmonotone height traces.
After tilting toward the endpoint, the positive and negative height
inventories are
\[
  P_+=\frac{z/\alpha}{1-z/\alpha},\qquad
  P_-=\frac{z\alpha}{1-z\alpha}.
\]
The resulting scalar supersolution discriminant is still negative at
\(z=M_q^{-1}\) for \(q=2,3,4,5,6\), becoming positive only at \(q=7\).  Thus
low-branching nonmonotone traces require structure beyond a two-sided row
sum: portal distinctness, adjacent-gap hard-core constraints, killed annular
excess, or mutual packet avoidance.

## Continuation update, Round 510, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 510 converts the low-branching nonmonotone obstruction into a finite
residual target.  With \(C=2\) and \(\alpha=0.99\), the monotone packet
contribution leaves explicit margins for \(q=2,\ldots,6\), starting with
\(0.690084\ldots\) at \(q=2\).  Since the crude two-sided tilted scalar
closure already becomes feasible at \(q=7\), the remaining scalar trace
problem is to bound reversal clusters for the finite set \(q=2,\ldots,6\)
using portal distinctness, adjacent-gap matchings, annular excess, and mutual
avoidance.

## Continuation update, Round 511, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 511 computes a neutral reversal-bubble inventory.  A bubble has two
raw \(z\)-weighted legs and, by the no-consecutive-vertical rule, at least one
child atom, giving
\[
  D_q(z)=\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^2
  \left((1+2c_q(z))^2-1\right),
  \qquad c_q(z)=qz^2w_*^2.
\]
At \(z=M_q^{-1}\), the geometric bubble correction
\(H\,D_q/(1-D_q)\) is smaller than the Round 510 margin for every
\(q=2,\ldots,6\).  If valid nonmonotone traces can be injected into monotone
traces decorated by such bubbles, the scalar packet transform closes for all
integer \(q\ge2\).

## Continuation update, Round 512, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 512 audits and repairs the Round 511 primitive-bubble count.  A
subdivided monotone leg can be collapsed to one atom without increasing the
majorant because \(2c_q(z)\le0.32<1\) for \(q=2,\ldots,6\) at
\(z=M_q^{-1}\).  This supports the primitive bubble inventory after
collapse.  The intended combinatorial map is now a record-envelope
decomposition: record increments form a monotone skeleton, and excursions
below a record level become neutral reversal bubbles, with child jumps that
cross a record split at a virtual cut.  The remaining work is to formalize
this for packet families and adjacent-gap vertical constraints.

## Continuation update, Round 513, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 513 states the single-trace scalar record-envelope lemma.  In the
residual cases, atoms carry weight \(z^{|\delta|}\) or
\(az^{|\delta|}\) with \(a=2qz^2w_*^2<1\).  The target lemma encodes every
trace from \(0\) to \(S>0\) as a monotone trace plus an ordered list of
neutral primitive bubbles with inventory
\[
  D_q(z)=\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^2((1+a)^2-1).
\]
The proposed proof uses running record maxima, virtual cuts of child atoms
at crossed record levels, and recursive reflection of excursions.  Proving
this rigorously would close the scalar one-trace residual; the packet-family
extension remains.

## Continuation update, Round 514, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 514 observes that the single-trace record-envelope lemma would lift to
packets.  With
\[
  B_q(z)=H_{q,z,0.99}(2)/(1-D_q(z)),
\]
one has \(B_q(M_q^{-1})<2\) for \(q=2,\ldots,6\), with worst value
\(1.742953\ldots\) at \(q=2\).  A packet of size \(k\) is therefore bounded
by \(B_q^k\le2^k\), while the child-label choices and recursive packet
factors are already included in the scalar atom cost.  The remaining issue
after the scalar packet lift is the ordered gap gas and compatibility of
virtual cuts with gap weights.

## Continuation update, Round 515, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 515 audits the span bookkeeping in Rounds 507--514.  In a same-scale
packet norm, a child atom contributes \(\alpha^{|h-h'|}\), not the extra
\(z^{|h-h'|}\) used in the scalar closure diagnostics.  Consequently monotone
child subdivisions leave an exponential composition factor, so the scalar
closure is not yet a same-norm fixed point.  The corrected target is a
two-scale Banach estimate
\[
  \mathcal T:\mathcal B_\theta\to\mathcal B_\alpha,\qquad \theta<\alpha,
\]
where child atoms carry \((\theta/\alpha)^{|h-h'|}\) and vertical atoms carry
\((z/\alpha)^{|h-h'|}\).  The previous scalar work remains useful as a
calibrated model for this two-scale estimate, but an additional bootstrap or
analytic-norm argument is required.

## Continuation update, Round 516, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 516 rewrites the residual scalar calculation in valid two-scale form.
With \(\theta=z\), \(\alpha=0.99\), and \(C=2\), child atoms use span ratio
\(\theta/\alpha\), vertical atoms use \(z/\alpha\), and neutral bubbles use
\(\theta\) for child legs and \(z\) for vertical legs.  The resulting values
of \(H/(1-D)\) for \(q=2,\ldots,6\) are
\[
  1.742953,\ 1.411562,\ 1.223666,\ 0.951638,\ 0.788235,
\]
all below \(2\).  This validates the residual scalar estimate as a
\(\mathcal B_z\to\mathcal B_{0.99}\) bound.  The open technical step is now
bootstrapping or otherwise obtaining control at the stronger span scale
\(\theta=z\).

## Continuation update, Round 517, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 517 shows that a span-only bootstrap is insufficient.  The exact
monotone scalar fixed point has effective span bases
\[
  0.974664,\ 0.948207,\ 0.925926,\ 0.829994,\ 0.753661
\]
for \(q=2,\ldots,6\) at \(z=M_q^{-1}\).  Starting a two-scale step just above
these bases leaves too little room before scale \(1\): for \(q=2,3,4,5\) the
two-scale transform denominator is already negative, and for \(q=6\) the
neutral bubble inventory is \(0.946516\ldots\).  The route therefore needs an
excess-sensitive or gap-sensitive norm rather than span decay alone.

## Continuation update, Round 518, 2026-07-06

No external literature refresh was performed in this round.  Internally,
Round 518 proposes the excess-sensitive correction.  Reversal bubbles below a
record height can only use the finite annular reserve below that record, and
portal distinctness should assign primitive bubbles to distinct reserve
levels.  This replaces the unconstrained factor \(1/(1-D)\) by
\((1+D)^e\), where \(e\) is the record's available excess.  Numerically,
\[
  w_*(z)(1+D_q(z))<1
\]
for \(q=2,\ldots,6\) at \(z=M_q^{-1}\), with worst value
\(0.841367\ldots\).  Thus the calibrated excess multiplier has enough room
to absorb a finite reservoir of reversal bubbles.  The remaining task is the
injection from record bubbles to distinct lower excess levels.
